Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Your city has n junctions. There are m one-way roads between the junctions. As a mayor of the city, you have to ensure the security of all the junctions.

To ensure the security, you have to build some police checkposts. Checkposts can only be built in a junction. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if either i = j or the police patrol car can go to j from i and then come back to i.

Building checkposts costs some money. As some areas of the city are more expensive than others, building checkpost at some junctions might cost more money than other junctions.

You have to determine the minimum possible money needed to ensure the security of all the junctions. Also you have to find the number of ways to ensure the security in minimum price and in addition in minimum number of checkposts. Two ways are different if any of the junctions contains a checkpost in one of them and do not contain in the other.


-----Input-----

In the first line, you will be given an integer n, number of junctions (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5). In the next line, n space-separated integers will be given. The i^{th} integer is the cost of building checkpost at the i^{th} junction (costs will be non-negative and will not exceed 10^9).

The next line will contain an integer m (0 ≤ m ≤ 3·10^5). And each of the next m lines contains two integers u_{i} and v_{i} (1 ≤ u_{i}, v_{i} ≤ n; u ≠ v). A pair u_{i}, v_{i} means, that there is a one-way road which goes from u_{i} to v_{i}. There will not be more than one road between two nodes in the same direction.


-----Output-----

Print two integers separated by spaces. The first one is the minimum possible money needed to ensure the security of all the junctions. And the second one is the number of ways you can ensure the security modulo 1000000007 (10^9 + 7).


-----Examples-----
Input
3
1 2 3
3
1 2
2 3
3 2

Output
3 1

Input
5
2 8 0 6 0
6
1 4
1 3
2 4
3 4
4 5
5 1

Output
8 2

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n junctions with one-way roads between them
2. We need to build police checkposts at some junctions to ensure security
3. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if:
   - i = j (same junction), OR
   - There's a path from i to j and back from j to i (i.e., a cycle or bidirectional path)
4. We want to minimize total cost, then minimize the number of checkposts
5. We need to output minimum cost and number of ways to achieve it

## Key Insights

1. A checkpost at junction i can protect all junctions that are in the same strongly connected component (SCC) as i
2. Two junctions are in the same SCC if there's a path from one to the other and back
3. We need to find all SCCs in the directed graph
4. For each SCC, we need to place at least one checkpost (to protect all nodes in that component)
5. The optimal strategy is to place checkposts in SCCs with minimum cost (to minimize total cost)
6. If multiple SCCs have the same minimum cost, we can choose any of them, so we multiply the ways accordingly

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the directed graph from input
2. Find all strongly connected components (SCCs) using Kosaraju's or Tarjan's algorithm
3. For each SCC, find the minimum cost among its nodes
4. Find the global minimum cost among all SCCs
5. Count how many SCCs have this minimum cost (these give us ways to achieve the minimum cost)
6. The answer is (total cost, number of ways)

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def find_scc(graph, n):
    """Find strongly connected components using Kosaraju's algorithm"""
    # Step 1: First DFS to get finish times
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    order = []
    
    def dfs1(node):
        visited[node] = True
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs1(neighbor)
        order.append(node)
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if not visited[i]:
            dfs1(i)
    
    # Step 2: Build reversed graph
    rev_graph = defaultdict(list)
    for u in graph:
        for v in graph[u]:
            rev_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 3: Second DFS on reverse graph
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    sccs = []
    
    def dfs2(node, component):
        visited[node] = True
        component.append(node)
        for neighbor in rev_graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs2(neighbor, component)
    
    for i in range(len(order) - 1, -1, -1):
        node = order[i]
        if not visited[node]:
            component = []
            dfs2(node, component)
            sccs.append(component)
    
    return sccs

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        graph[u].append(v)
    
    # Find SCCs
    sccs = find_scc(graph, n)
    
    # For each SCC, find minimum cost node
    min_costs = []
    for scc in sccs:
        min_cost = min(costs[node - 1] for node in scc)  # costs are 0-indexed
        min_costs.append(min_cost)
    
    # Find global minimum cost
    min_total_cost = min(min_costs)
    
    # Count number of ways (how many SCCs have minimum cost)
    ways = 0
    for cost in min_costs:
        if cost == min_total_cost:
            ways += 1
    
    print(min_total_cost * ways, ways % 1000000007)

solve()
```

For {2}: cost = 8

Ways: only one way (we must pick the SCC with cost 0, and must pick SCC with cost 8)

But output says 8 2. 

Hmm, maybe there's another interpretation. 

Wait, maybe "minimum price" means we want to minimize cost, but if multiple options have same cost, we count them. 

For the SCC {1,3,4,5}, we have nodes with costs [2,0,6,0]. The minimum cost is 0. There are two nodes (3 and 5) with cost 0. 

So we have 2 choices for which node to place the checkpost in this component.

For the SCC {2}, only one node with cost 8.

Total cost = 8 (from {2}) + 0 (from {1,3,4,5}) = 8
Number of ways = 2 (choices for which node in {1,3,4,5} has cost 0)

This matches the expected output: 8 2.

I see! The key insight is:
1. Each SCC requires exactly one checkpost (to protect all nodes in that component)
2. For each SCC, we choose the node with minimum cost (or any node with minimum cost if there are multiple)
3. The total cost is sum of minimum costs across all SCCs
4. The number of ways is the product of (number of nodes with minimum cost in each SCC)

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def find_scc(graph, n):
    """Find strongly connected components using Kosaraju's algorithm"""
    # Step 1: First DFS to get finish times
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    order = []
    
    def dfs1(node):
        visited[node] = True
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs1(neighbor)
        order.append(node)
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        if not visited[i]:
            dfs1(i)
    
    # Build reversed graph
    rev_graph = defaultdict(list)
    for u in graph:
        for v in graph[u]:
            rev_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 2: Second DFS on reverse graph
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    sccs = []
    
    def dfs2(node, component):
        visited[node] = True
        component.append(node)
        for neighbor in rev_graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs2(neighbor, component)
    
    for i in range(len(order) - 1, -1, -1):
        node = order[i]
        if not visited[node]:
            component = []
            dfs2(node, component)
            sccs.append(component)
    
    return sccs

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        graph[u].append(v)
    
    # Find SCCs
    sccs = find_scc(graph, n)
    
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    
    # For each SCC, find minimum cost and count how many nodes have that cost
    for scc in sccs:
        min_cost = float('inf')
        count_min = 0
        
        for node in scc:
            cost_val = costs[node - 1]  # Convert to 0-indexed cost
            if cost_val < min_cost:
                min_cost = cost_val
                count_min = 1
            elif cost_val == min_cost:
                count_min += 1
        
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * count_min) % 1000000007
    
    print(total_cost, ways)

solve()
```

    """Find strongly connected components using Kosaraju's algorithm"""

            dfs1(i)

    rev_graph = defaultdict(list)
    for u in graph:
        for v in graph[u]:
            rev_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 2: Second DFS on reverse graph
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    sccs = []
    
    def dfs2(node, component):
        visited[node] = True
        component.append(node)
        for neighbor in rev_graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs2(neighbor, component)
    
    for i in range(len(order) - 1, -1, -1):
        node = order[i]
        if not visited[node]:
            component = []
            dfs2(node, component)
            sccs.append(component)
    
    return sccs

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build graph
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())

    sccs = find_scc(graph, n)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Your city has n junctions. There are m one-way roads between the junctions. As a mayor of the city, you have to ensure the security of all the junctions.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n junctions with one-way roads
2. We need to build police checkposts at some junctions
3. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if:
   - i = j (same junction), OR
   - there's a path from i to j and back from j to i (i.e., j is in the same strongly connected component as i)
4. We want to minimize total cost, and among minimum cost solutions, we want to minimize the number of checkposts
5. We need to output: minimum cost and number of ways to achieve it

## Key Insights

1. A checkpost at junction i protects all junctions in the strongly connected component (SCC) that contains i
2. We need to find all SCCs in the directed graph
3. For each SCC, we can place a checkpost at any junction in that component, and it will protect all junctions in that component
4. To minimize cost, we should place a checkpost at the junction with minimum cost in each SCC
5. To minimize the number of checkposts, we need exactly one checkpost per SCC

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all strongly connected components (SCCs) using Kosaraju's or Tarjan's algorithm
2. For each SCC, find the minimum cost junction in that component
3. Sum up the minimum costs of all SCCs to get total minimum cost
4. Count the number of ways: if there are multiple junctions with the same minimum cost in an SCC, multiply the count of such junctions across all SCCs

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    rev_graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        v -= 1
        graph[u].append(v)
        rev_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 1: Find SCCs using Kosaraju's algorithm
    def dfs1(node, visited, stack):
        visited[node] = True
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs1(neighbor, visited, stack)
        stack.append(node)
    
    def dfs2(node, visited, component):
        visited[node] = True
        component.append(node)
        for neighbor in rev_graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs2(neighbor, visited, component)
    
    # First DFS to get order
    visited = [False] * n
    stack = []
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            dfs1(i, visited, stack)
    
    # Second DFS on transpose graph
    visited = [False] * n
    sccs = []
    
    while stack:
        node = stack.pop()
        if not visited[node]:
            component = []
            dfs2(node, visited, component)
            sccs.append(component)
    
    # Step 2: For each SCC, find minimum cost and count of minimum cost vertices
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    
    for component in sccs:
        min_cost = float('inf')
        min_cost_count = 0
        
        for node in component:
            if costs[node] < min_cost:
                min_cost = costs[node]
                min_cost_count = 1
            elif costs[node] == min_cost:
                min_cost_count += 1
        
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * min_cost_count) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost, ways)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the solution:

1. **Kosaraju's Algorithm**: 
   - First DFS gives us the order of nodes by finishing times
   - Second DFS on transpose graph identifies SCCs

2. **Cost calculation**:
   - For each SCC, we find the minimum cost among all nodes in that component
   - This minimum cost will be our contribution to the total minimum cost

3. **Ways calculation**:
   - If multiple nodes in an SCC have the same minimum cost, we can choose any of them
   - The total number of ways is the product of the counts of minimum-cost nodes across all SCCs

4. **Edge cases**:
   - Single node SCCs: cost is just the cost of that node
   - Multiple nodes with same minimum cost: we have multiple choices

The time complexity is O(n + m) for SCC detection and O(n) for processing components, which is efficient for the given constraints.

from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    rev_graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        v -= 1
        graph[u].append(v)
        rev_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 1: Find SCCs using Kosaraju's algorithm
    def dfs1(node, visited, stack):
        visited[node] = True
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs1(neighbor, visited, stack)
        stack.append(node)
    
    def dfs2(node, visited, component):
        visited[node] = True
        component.append(node)
        for neighbor in rev_graph[node]:
            if not visited[neighbor]:
                dfs2(neighbor, visited, component)
    
    # First DFS to get order
    visited = [False] * n
    stack = []
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            dfs1(i, visited, stack)
    
    # Second DFS on transpose graph
    visited = [False] * n
    sccs = []
    
    while stack:
        node = stack.pop()
        if not visited[node]:
            component = []
            dfs2(node, visited, component)
            sccs.append(component)
    
    # Step 2: For each SCC, find minimum cost and count of minimum cost vertices
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    
    for component in sccs:
        min_cost = float('inf')
        min_cost_count = 0
        
        for node in component:
            if costs[node] < min_cost:
                min_cost = costs[node]
                min_cost_count = 1
            elif costs[node] == min_cost:
                min_cost_count += 1
        
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * min_cost_count) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost, ways)

solve()

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n junctions with one-way roads between them
2. We need to build police checkposts at some junctions to secure all junctions
3. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if:
   - i = j (same junction), OR
   - There's a path from i to j AND from j back to i (i.e., j is in the same strongly connected component as i)
4. We want to minimize total cost, and then count the number of ways to achieve this minimum cost with minimum number of checkposts

## Key Insights

1. A checkpost at a junction protects all junctions in its strongly connected component (SCC)
2. To minimize checkposts, we should place checkposts in SCCs that have the minimum cost
3. The minimum cost will be achieved by selecting the minimum cost vertex from each SCC
4. If multiple vertices in an SCC have the same minimum cost, we have multiple ways to choose

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all strongly connected components (SCCs) using Kosaraju's or Tarjan's algorithm
2. For each SCC, find the minimum cost vertex and count how many vertices have that minimum cost
3. Sum up the minimum costs across all SCCs
4. Multiply the number of choices (ways) for each SCC to get the total number of ways

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    rev_graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        v -= 1
        graph[u].append(v)
        rev_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 1: First DFS to get finishing times
    visited = [False] * n
    order = []
    
    def dfs1(u):
        visited[u] = True
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                dfs1(v)
        order.append(u)
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            dfs1(i)
    
    # Step 2: Second DFS on reverse graph to find SCCs
    visited = [False] * n
    sccs = []
    
    def dfs2(u, scc):
        visited[u] = True
        scc.append(u)
        for v in rev_graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                dfs2(v, scc)
    
    # Process in reverse order of finishing times
    while order:
        u = order.pop()
        if not visited[u]:
            scc = []
            dfs2(u, scc)
            sccs.append(scc)
    
    # Step 3: For each SCC, find minimum cost and count occurrences
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    
    for scc in sccs:
        min_cost = min(costs[i] for i in scc)
        # Count how many vertices in this SCC have the minimum cost
        count_min = sum(1 for i in scc if costs[i] == min_cost)
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * count_min) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost, ways)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Solution

1. **Strongly Connected Components (SCCs)**: 
   - Use Kosaraju's algorithm to find all SCCs
   - First DFS: get nodes in order of finishing time
   - Second DFS on reverse graph: group nodes into SCCs

2. **Cost Calculation**:
   - For each SCC, the minimum cost vertex will serve as a checkpost
   - The total cost is the sum of minimum costs across all SCCs

3. **Count of Ways**:
   - If multiple vertices in an SCC have the same minimum cost, we have multiple ways to choose
   - Multiply the number of choices from each SCC to get total ways

4. **Edge Cases**:
   - Single node SCCs are handled naturally
   - Multiple nodes with same minimum cost in an SCC contribute to ways calculation

The time complexity is O(n + m) for SCC detection and O(n) for cost calculation, which is efficient for the given constraints.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Your city has n junctions. There are m one-way roads between the junctions. As a mayor of the city, you have to ensure the security of all the junctions.

To ensure the security, you have to build some police checkposts. Checkposts can only be built in a junction. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if either i = j or the police patrol car can go to j from i and then come back to i.

Building checkposts costs some money. As some areas of the city are more expensive than others, building checkpost at some junctions might cost more money than other junctions.

You have to determine the minimum possible money needed to ensure the security of all the junctions. Also you have to find the number of ways to ensure the security in minimum price and in addition in minimum number of checkposts. Two ways are different if any of the junctions contains a checkpost in one of them and do not contain in the other.


-----Input-----

In the first line, you will be given an integer n, number of junctions (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5). In the next line, n space-separated integers will be given. The i^{th} integer is the cost of building checkpost at the i^{th} junction (costs will be non-negative and will not exceed 10^9).

The next line will contain an integer m (0 ≤ m ≤ 3·10^5). And each of the next m lines contains two integers u_{i} and v_{i} (1 ≤ u_{i}, v_{i} ≤ n; u ≠ v). A pair u_{i}, v_{i} means, that there is a one-way road which goes from u_{i} to v_{i}. There will not be more than one road between two nodes in the same direction.


-----Output-----

Print two integers separated by spaces. The first one is the minimum possible money needed to ensure the security of all the junctions. And the second one is the number of ways you can ensure the security modulo 1000000007 (10^9 + 7).


-----Examples-----

1 2 3

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n junctions with one-way roads between them
2. We need to build police checkposts at some junctions to ensure security
3. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if:
   - i = j (same junction), OR
   - There's a cycle that goes from i to j and back to i
4. We want to minimize total cost, and among solutions with minimum cost, we want to minimize the number of checkposts
5. We need to output: (minimum cost, number of ways)

## Key Insights

1. A checkpost at junction i can protect all junctions that are in the same strongly connected component (SCC) as i
2. If we have a cycle, any junction in that cycle can protect all other junctions in the cycle
3. We need to find all SCCs in the directed graph
4. For each SCC, we can place a checkpost at any junction in that component, and it will protect all junctions in that component
5. The optimal strategy is to place exactly one checkpost per SCC (to minimize the number of checkposts)
6. For each SCC, we choose the junction with minimum cost to minimize total cost
7. If multiple junctions in an SCC have the same minimum cost, we multiply the number of ways

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the directed graph from input
2. Find all strongly connected components (SCCs) using Tarjan's or Kosaraju's algorithm
3. For each SCC, find the minimum cost among all junctions in it
4. Sum up the minimum costs to get total minimum cost
5. Count the number of ways: for each SCC, if there are k junctions with minimum cost, multiply by k
6. Return (total_cost, ways % 1000000007)

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        v -= 1
        graph[u].append(v)
    
    # Find strongly connected components using Kosaraju's algorithm
    def kosaraju():
        # Step 1: First DFS to get finishing times
        visited = [False] * n
        order = []
        
        def dfs1(node):
            visited[node] = True
            for neighbor in graph[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    dfs1(neighbor)
            order.append(node)
        
        for i in range(n):
            if not visited[i]:
                dfs1(i)
        
        # Step 2: Build transpose graph
        transpose = [[] for _ in range(n)]
        for u in range(n):
            for v in graph[u]:
                transpose[v].append(u)
        
        # Step 3: Second DFS on transpose graph
        visited = [False] * n
        sccs = []
        
        def dfs2(node, component):
            visited[node] = True
            component.append(node)
            for neighbor in transpose[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    dfs2(neighbor, component)
        
        for i in range(n - 1, -1, -1):
            node = order[i]
            if not visited[node]:
                component = []
                dfs2(node, component)
                sccs.append(component)
        
        return sccs
    
    # Get SCCs
    sccs = kosaraju()
    
    # Calculate minimum cost and number of ways
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    for scc in sccs:
        # Find minimum cost in this SCC
        min_cost = min(costs[node] for node in scc)
        
        # Count how many nodes have this minimum cost
        count_min_cost = sum(1 for node in scc if costs[node] == min_cost)
        
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * count_min_cost) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost, ways)

solve()
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1**: n=3, costs=[1,2,3], roads: 1→2, 2→3, 3→2
- SCCs: {0}, {1,2} (since 1→2→3→2 forms cycle)
- Component {0}: min cost = 1, count = 1
- Component {1,2}: min cost = 2, count = 1 
- Total cost = 1 + 2 = 3, ways = 1 × 1 = 1 ✓

**Example 2**: n=5, costs=[2,8,0,6,0], roads form cycles
- Need to identify SCCs properly - the graph has cycles among nodes
- Wait, let me trace: 1→4, 1→3, 2→4, 3→4, 4→5, 5→1
- This forms one cycle: 1→4→5→1, and nodes 2,3 pointing to 4
- SCCs: {0,3,4} (since 1→4→5→1 forms cycle), {1}, {2}
- Component {0,3,4}: min cost = min(2,6,0) = 0, count = 2 (nodes 2 and 4 have cost 0)
- Component {1}: min cost = 8, count = 1
- Component {2}: min cost = 0, count = 1
- Total cost = 0 + 8 + 0 = 8, ways = 2 × 1 × 1 = 2 ✓

The algorithm looks correct. Let me finalize:

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Your city has n junctions. There are m one-way roads between the junctions. As a mayor of the city, you have to ensure the security of all the junctions.

To ensure the security, you have to build some police checkposts. Checkposts can only be built in a junction. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if either i = j or the police patrol car can go to j from i and then come back to i.

The next line will contain an integer m (0 ≤ m ≤ 3·10^5). And each of the next m lines contains two integers u_{i} and v_{i} (1 ≤ u_{i}, v_{i} ≤ n; u ≠ v). A pair u_{i}, v_{i} means, that there is a one-way road which goes from u_{i} to v_{i}. There will not be more than one road between two nodes in the same direction.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n junctions connected by m one-way roads
2. We need to build police checkposts at some junctions to ensure security
3. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if:
   - i = j (same junction), OR
   - There's a cycle that goes from i to j and back to i (i.e., j is in the same strongly connected component as i)
4. We want to minimize the total cost, and if there are multiple ways with the same minimum cost, we count the number of ways
5. We also want to minimize the number of checkposts

## Key Insights

1. A checkpost at a junction i protects all junctions in the same strongly connected component (SCC) as i
2. The key insight is that we only need one checkpost per strongly connected component
3. We should place checkposts in SCCs with minimum cost to minimize total cost
4. For the number of ways: if multiple junctions in an SCC have the same minimum cost, we can choose any of them

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all strongly connected components (SCCs) using Kosaraju's or Tarjan's algorithm
2. For each SCC, find the minimum cost among all junctions in that component
3. The total minimum cost is the sum of minimum costs of all SCCs
4. The number of ways is the product of the number of junctions in each SCC that have the minimum cost for that SCC

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    reversed_graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        v -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        graph[u].append(v)
        reversed_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 1: Find all strongly connected components using Kosaraju's algorithm
    def get_sccs():
        # First DFS to get finish order
        visited = [False] * n
        finish_order = []
        
        def dfs1(node):
            visited[node] = True
            for neighbor in graph[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    dfs1(neighbor)
            finish_order.append(node)
        
        for i in range(n):
            if not visited[i]:
                dfs1(i)
        
        # Second DFS on reversed graph
        visited = [False] * n
        sccs = []
        
        def dfs2(node, scc):
            visited[node] = True
            scc.append(node)
            for neighbor in reversed_graph[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    dfs2(neighbor, scc)
        
        # Process in reverse finish order
        for node in reversed(finish_order):
            if not visited[node]:
                scc = []
                dfs2(node, scc)
                sccs.append(scc)
        
        return sccs
    
    # Get SCCs
    sccs = get_sccs()
    
    # For each SCC, find minimum cost and count of junctions with that cost
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    
    for scc in sccs:
        if not scc:
            continue
            
        # Find minimum cost in this SCC
        min_cost = min(costs[i] for i in scc)
        
        # Count how many junctions in this SCC have the minimum cost
        count_min_cost = sum(1 for i in scc if costs[i] == min_cost)
        
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * count_min_cost) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost, ways)

solve()
```

## Verification with Examples

Let me trace through the first example:
- Junctions: 1, 2, 3 with costs [1, 2, 3]
- Roads: 1→2, 2→3, 3→2

SCC analysis:
- Junctions 2 and 3 form a cycle: 2→3→2
- Junction 1 is isolated
- SCCs: [1], [2,3]

For SCC [1]: min_cost = 1, count = 1
For SCC [2,3]: min_cost = 2, count = 1 (only junction 2 has cost 2, junction 3 has cost 3)

Total cost = 1 + 2 = 3
Number of ways = 1 × 1 = 1

This matches the expected output: "3 1"

The algorithm correctly identifies SCCs and computes the minimum cost and number of ways.

from collections import defaultdict, deque
import sys

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    reversed_graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        v -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        graph[u].append(v)
        reversed_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 1: Find all strongly connected components using Kosaraju's algorithm
    def get_sccs():
        # First DFS to get finish order
        visited = [False] * n
        finish_order = []
        
        def dfs1(node):
            visited[node] = True
            for neighbor in graph[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    dfs1(neighbor)
            finish_order.append(node)
        
        for i in range(n):
            if not visited[i]:
                dfs1(i)
        
        # Second DFS on reversed graph
        visited = [False] * n
        sccs = []
        
        def dfs2(node, scc):
            visited[node] = True
            scc.append(node)
            for neighbor in reversed_graph[node]:
                if not visited[neighbor]:
                    dfs2(neighbor, scc)
        
        # Process in reverse finish order
        for node in reversed(finish_order):
            if not visited[node]:
                scc = []
                dfs2(node, scc)
                sccs.append(scc)
        
        return sccs
    
    # Get SCCs
    sccs = get_sccs()
    
    # For each SCC, find minimum cost and count of junctions with that cost
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    
    for scc in sccs:
        if not scc:
            continue
            
        # Find minimum cost in this SCC
        min_cost = min(costs[i] for i in scc)
        
        # Count how many junctions in this SCC have the minimum cost
        count_min_cost = sum(1 for i in scc if costs[i] == min_cost)
        
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * count_min_cost) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost, ways)

solve()

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Your city has n junctions. There are m one-way roads between the junctions. As a mayor of the city, you have to ensure the security of all the junctions.

To ensure the security, you have to build some police checkposts. Checkposts can only be built in a junction. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if either i = j or the police patrol car can go to j from i and then come back to i.

Building checkposts costs some money. As some areas of the city are more expensive than others, building checkpost at some junctions might cost more money than other junctions.

You have to determine the minimum possible money needed to ensure the security of all the junctions. Also you have to find the number of ways to ensure the security in minimum price and in addition in minimum number of checkposts. Two ways are different if any of the junctions contains a checkpost in one of them and do not contain in the other.


-----Input-----

In the first line, you will be given an integer n, number of junctions (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5). In the next line, n space-separated integers will be given. The i^{th} integer is the cost of building checkpost at the i^{th} junction (costs will be non-negative and will not exceed 10^9).

The next line will contain an integer m (0 ≤ m ≤ 3·10^5). And each of the next m lines contains two integers u_{i} and v_{i} (1 ≤ u_{i}, v_{i} ≤ n; u ≠ v). A pair u_{i}, v_{i} means, that there is a one-way road which goes from u_{i} to v_{i}. There will not be more than one road between two nodes in the same direction.


-----Output-----

Print two integers separated by spaces. The first one is the minimum possible money needed to ensure the security of all the junctions. And the second one is the number of ways you can ensure the security modulo 1000000007 (10^9 + 7).


-----Examples-----

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n junctions with one-way roads between them
2. We need to build police checkposts at some junctions to ensure security
3. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if there's a path from i to j and back from j to i (i.e., a cycle or direct connection)
4. Each junction has a cost to build a checkpost
5. We need to minimize total cost while ensuring all junctions are protected
6. We also need to count the number of ways to achieve minimum cost with minimum checkposts

## Key Insights

1. A checkpost at junction i can protect all junctions that are reachable from i and can reach back to i (i.e., in the same strongly connected component)
2. For each strongly connected component (SCC), we only need to build one checkpost to protect all junctions in that component
3. The optimal solution is to pick the cheapest junction in each SCC to build the checkpost
4. We need to find all SCCs in the directed graph

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all strongly connected components (SCC) using Kosaraju's or Tarjan's algorithm
2. For each SCC, find the minimum cost junction in that component
3. Sum the minimum costs across all SCCs to get the total minimum cost
4. Count the number of ways: for each SCC, if there are multiple junctions with the same minimum cost, multiply the count by the number of such junctions

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    import sys
    from collections import defaultdict, deque
    
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    # Parse input
    n = int(data[0])
    costs = list(map(int, data[1:n+1]))
    
    m = int(data[n+1])
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    rev_graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    index = n + 2
    for _ in range(m):
        u = int(data[index]) - 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        v = int(data[index + 1]) - 1
        index += 2
        graph[u].append(v)
        rev_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 1: First DFS to get finishing times
    visited = [False] * n
    finish_order = []
    
    def dfs1(u):
        visited[u] = True
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                dfs1(v)
        finish_order.append(u)
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            dfs1(i)
    
    # Step 2: Second DFS on reverse graph to find SCCs
    visited_scc = [False] * n
    scc_components = []
    
    def dfs2(u, component):
        visited_scc[u] = True
        component.append(u)
        for v in rev_graph[u]:
            if not visited_scc[v]:
                dfs2(v, component)
    
    # Process in reverse order of finish times
    for i in range(len(finish_order) - 1, -1, -1):
        u = finish_order[i]
        if not visited_scc[u]:
            component = []
            dfs2(u, component)
            scc_components.append(component)
    
    # Step 3: For each SCC, find minimum cost and count of minimum cost vertices
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    mod = 1000000007
    
    for component in scc_components:
        min_cost = float('inf')
        min_count = 0
        
        for node in component:
            if costs[node] < min_cost:
                min_cost = costs[node]
                min_count = 1
            elif costs[node] == min_cost:
                min_count += 1
        
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * min_count) % mod
    
    print(total_cost, ways)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Solution

1. **SCC Detection**: I use Kosaraju's algorithm which consists of:
   - First DFS to get nodes in order of finishing times
   - Second DFS on the reverse graph to find strongly connected components

2. **Minimum Cost Calculation**: For each SCC, I find the minimum cost among all nodes in that component. This is because one checkpost in any node of the SCC will protect all nodes in that component.

3. **Way Counting**: For each SCC, if multiple nodes have the same minimum cost, each such node represents a different way to place the checkpost. I multiply these counts together.

4. **Complexity**: O(V + E) for SCC detection and O(V) for processing components, which is efficient for the given constraints.

The solution handles all edge cases including:
- Disconnected graphs (each node is its own SCC)
- Cycles (all nodes in a cycle form one SCC)
- Multiple minimum cost nodes in the same SCC
- No roads (each node is its own SCC)

The time complexity is O(n + m) and space complexity is O(n + m), which is efficient for the given constraints.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n junctions with one-way roads between them
2. We need to build police checkposts at some junctions to ensure security
3. A checkpost at junction i can protect junction j if:
   - i = j (same junction), OR
   - there's a path from i to j and back from j to i (i can reach j and j can reach i)
4. Each junction has a cost to build a checkpost
5. We need to minimize total cost and then count the number of ways to achieve this minimum cost with minimum number of checkposts

## Key Insights

1. A checkpost at junction i protects all junctions that are in the same strongly connected component (SCC) as i, because for any junction j in the same SCC, we can go from i to j and back from j to i.

2. Therefore, we need to find all strongly connected components (SCCs) in the directed graph.

3. For each SCC, we only need to build one checkpost (to protect all junctions in that component), and we should place it at the junction with minimum cost within that component.

4. The minimum cost will be the sum of minimum costs from each SCC.

5. For the number of ways: if there are multiple junctions in an SCC with the same minimum cost, each of them can serve as the checkpost location (so we multiply the count of minimum-cost junctions across all SCCs).

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Find all strongly connected components using Kosaraju's algorithm or Tarjan's algorithm
3. For each SCC:
   - Find the minimum cost among all junctions in that component
   - Count how many junctions have this minimum cost
4. Sum up all minimum costs to get total minimum cost
5. Multiply all counts to get the number of ways
6. Return the results

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    rev_graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        v -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        graph[u].append(v)
        rev_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 1: Find SCCs using Kosaraju's algorithm
    # First DFS to get finishing times
    visited = [False] * n
    order = []
    
    def dfs1(u):
        visited[u] = True
        for v in graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                dfs1(v)
        order.append(u)
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            dfs1(i)
    
    # Second DFS on reverse graph
    visited = [False] * n
    sccs = []
    
    def dfs2(u, component):
        visited[u] = True
        component.append(u)
        for v in rev_graph[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                dfs2(v, component)
    
    # Process in reverse order of finishing times
    for i in range(len(order) - 1, -1, -1):
        u = order[i]
        if not visited[u]:
            component = []
            dfs2(u, component)
            sccs.append(component)
    
    # Step 2: Calculate minimum cost and count for each SCC
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    for scc in sccs:
        # Find minimum cost in this SCC
        min_cost = float('inf')
        min_count = 0
        
        for node in scc:
            if costs[node] < min_cost:
                min_cost = costs[node]
                min_count = 1
            elif costs[node] == min_cost:
                min_count += 1
        
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * min_count) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost, ways)
```

Example 1: n=3, costs=[1,2,3], edges: 1→2, 2→3, 3→2
- SCCs: {1}, {2,3} 
- {1}: min cost = 1, count = 1
- {2,3}: min cost = 2, count = 1 (since cost[1]=2, cost[2]=3, cost[3]=3, so min is 2 at node 1, but node 1 is not in {2,3})
Wait, let me recalculate:

Junctions: 1,2,3 with costs 1,2,3
Edges: 1→2, 2→3, 3→2

In component {2,3}: costs are 2,3. Minimum cost is 2 at junction 2.
So total cost = 1 + 2 = 3, ways = 1 × 1 = 1 ✓

Example 2: n=5, costs=[2,8,0,6,0], edges as given
- Need to find SCCs...

   - i = j (same junction), OR
   - There's a cycle from i to j and back to i (i can reach j and j can reach i)
4. We want to minimize total cost, and if there are multiple ways with the same minimum cost, we want the count of ways
5. We also want to minimize the number of checkposts

## Key Insights

1. A checkpost at junction i can protect all junctions that are in the same strongly connected component (SCC) as i
2. Two junctions are in the same SCC if there's a path from one to the other and back
3. We need to find all SCCs in the directed graph
4. For each SCC, we can place one checkpost at the minimum cost junction within that SCC
5. The total cost is the sum of minimum costs from each SCC
6. The number of ways is the product of the number of minimum-cost junctions in each SCC

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the directed graph from input
2. Find all strongly connected components (SCCs) using Kosaraju's algorithm
3. For each SCC, find the minimum cost among all junctions in that component
4. Sum up these minimum costs for the total cost
5. For each SCC, count how many junctions have the minimum cost in that component
6. Multiply all these counts to get the total number of ways

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    MOD = 1000000007
    
    n = int(input())
    costs = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    m = int(input())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    rev_graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(m):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1  # Convert to 0-indexed
        v -= 1
        graph[u].append(v)
        rev_graph[v].append(u)
    
    # Step 1: First DFS to get finishing times
    visited = [False] * n
    order = []
    
    def dfs1(v):
        visited[v] = True
        for u in graph[v]:
            if not visited[u]:
                dfs1(u)
        order.append(v)
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            dfs1(i)
    
    # Step 2: Second DFS on reversed graph to find SCCs
    visited = [False] * n
    sccs = []
    
    def dfs2(v, component):
        visited[v] = True
        component.append(v)
        for u in rev_graph[v]:
            if not visited[u]:
                dfs2(u, component)
    
    # Process nodes in reverse order of finishing times
    for i in range(len(order) - 1, -1, -1):
        node = order[i]
        if not visited[node]:
            component = []
            dfs2(node, component)
            sccs.append(component)
    
    # Step 3: For each SCC, find minimum cost and count of minimum cost junctions
    total_cost = 0
    ways = 1
    
    for scc in sccs:
        min_cost = float('inf')
        min_count = 0
        
        for node in scc:
            if costs[node] < min_cost:
                min_cost = costs[node]
                min_count = 1
            elif costs[node] == min_cost:
                min_count += 1
        
        total_cost += min_cost
        ways = (ways * min_count) % MOD
    
    print(total_cost, ways)

solve()
```

## Explanation of the Algorithm

1. **Kosaraju's Algorithm**: 
   - First DFS: Find the order of vertices by finishing times
   - Second DFS on reversed graph: Find SCCs in order of decreasing finishing times

2. **For each SCC**:
   - Find the minimum cost among all junctions in that component
   - Count how many junctions have this minimum cost
   - Add minimum cost to total cost
   - Multiply count by total ways

3. **Why this works**:
   - Within an SCC, any junction can protect all other junctions in the same component (since there's a path from any node to any other node and back)
   - So we only need one checkpost per SCC
   - We place it at the junction with minimum cost to minimize total cost
   - Multiple junctions with the same minimum cost in an SCC give us multiple ways to achieve the minimum cost

The time complexity is O(V + E) for finding SCCs, and the space complexity is O(V + E).

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the smallest integer x that is:
1. Greater than n (x > n)
2. Divisible by k (x % k == 0)

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** n=5, k=3
- We need x > 5 and x divisible by 3
- Multiples of 3: 3, 6, 9, 12, ...
- The first multiple of 3 that's greater than 5 is 6

**Example 2:** n=25, k=13
- We need x > 25 and x divisible by 13
- Multiples of 13: 13, 26, 39, ...
- The first multiple of 13 that's greater than 25 is 26

**Example 3:** n=26, k=13
- We need x > 26 and x divisible by 13
- The next multiple of 13 after 26 is 39

## Algorithm Development

The key insight is that we need the next multiple of k that is greater than n.

1. If n is already divisible by k, then the next multiple is (n // k + 1) * k
2. Otherwise, the next multiple is (n // k + 1) * k

Wait, let me think more carefully:

- If n = 5, k = 3: 5 // 3 = 1, so (1 + 1) * 3 = 6 ✓
- If n = 25, k = 13: 25 // 13 = 1, so (1 + 1) * 13 = 26 ✓  
- If n = 26, k = 13: 26 // 13 = 2, so (2 + 1) * 13 = 39 ✓

Actually, the formula works for all cases: the next multiple of k after n is `(n // k + 1) * k`.

But let me verify edge cases:
- If n = 0, k = 5: (0 // 5 + 1) * 5 = 5 ✓
- If n = 4, k = 5: (4 // 5 + 1) * 5 = (0 + 1) * 5 = 5 ✓

The formula `(n // k + 1) * k` gives us the smallest multiple of k that is greater than n.

## Why this works

When we divide n by k and take the floor (integer division), we get the quotient of n/k. The next multiple of k is obtained by taking this quotient, adding 1, and multiplying by k.

For example:
- n = 5, k = 3: 5 // 3 = 1, (1 + 1) * 3 = 6
- n = 25, k = 13: 25 // 13 = 1, (1 + 1) * 13 = 26
- n = 26, k = 13: 26 // 13 = 2, (2 + 1) * 13 = 39

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
x = (n // k + 1) * k
print(x)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the smallest integer x that is:
1. Greater than n (x > n)
2. Divisible by k (x % k == 0)

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me think about this mathematically:

1. We want the smallest multiple of k that is greater than n.
2. The multiples of k are: k, 2k, 3k, 4k, ...
3. We need to find the smallest multiple of k that is > n.

## Mathematical Approach

If we divide n by k, we get:
- n ÷ k = q with remainder r (where 0 ≤ r < k)
- This means n = q × k + r

The next multiple of k after n would be:
- If r = 0 (n is already divisible by k), then the next multiple is (q + 1) × k
- If r > 0 (n is not divisible by k), then the next multiple is (q + 1) × k

Wait, let me reconsider this more carefully.

Actually, the next multiple of k after n is:
- If n is divisible by k, then n + k is the next one
- If n is not divisible by k, then we just need to find the next multiple

But there's a simpler way: the smallest multiple of k that is greater than n is:
- (n // k + 1) * k

Let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1: n=5, k=3**
- 5 // 3 = 1
- (1 + 1) * 3 = 2 * 3 = 6 ✓

**Example 2: n=25, k=13**
- 25 // 13 = 1
- (1 + 1) * 13 = 2 * 13 = 26 ✓

**Example 3: n=26, k=13**
- 26 // 13 = 2
- (2 + 1) * 13 = 3 * 13 = 39 ✓

Perfect! This works for all cases.

## Algorithm

1. Read n and k
2. Calculate x = (n // k + 1) * k
3. Output x

## Edge Cases

- If n is already divisible by k, (n // k + 1) * k = (q + 1) * k, which is the correct next multiple.
- The formula works for all valid inputs since n, k ≥ 1.

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
x = (n // k + 1) * k
print(x)
```

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the smallest integer x such that:
1. x > n (x is greater than n)
2. x is divisible by k (x % k == 0)

## Approach Analysis

Let me think about this mathematically:

If we have a number n and want the next multiple of k that's greater than n:

- The multiples of k are: k, 2k, 3k, 4k, ...
- We need to find the smallest multiple of k that is greater than n.

## Mathematical Solution

Let's say n = q*k + r (where q is quotient and r is remainder when n is divided by k).

There are two cases:
1. If r = 0 (n is already divisible by k), then the next multiple is (q+1)*k
2. If r > 0 (n is not divisible by k), then the next multiple is (q+1)*k

Actually, in both cases, we can use: `(n // k + 1) * k`

Let me verify with examples:

**Example 1: n=5, k=3**
- 5 // 3 = 1 (quotient)
- (1 + 1) * 3 = 2 * 3 = 6 ✓

**Example 2: n=25, k=13**
- 25 // 13 = 1 (quotient)
- (1 + 1) * 13 = 2 * 13 = 26 ✓

**Example 3: n=26, k=13**
- 26 // 13 = 2 (quotient)
- (2 + 1) * 13 = 3 * 13 = 39 ✓

Perfect! The formula works for all cases.

## Edge Cases

What if n is already divisible by k? Like n=13, k=13:
- 13 // 13 = 1
- (1 + 1) * 13 = 26 ✓ (which is correct - next multiple after 13)

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
x = (n // k + 1) * k
print(x)
```

The solution is straightforward:
1. Read n and k from input
2. Calculate the next multiple of k that is greater than n using the formula `(n // k + 1) * k`
3. Print the result

This works because:
- `n // k` gives us the quotient when n is divided by k
- Adding 1 ensures we get the next multiple
- Multiplying by k gives us the actual value of that multiple

Time complexity: O(1)
Space complexity: O(1)

1. Greater than n (x > n)

## Mathematical Approach

- n ÷ k = quotient + remainder

The remainder tells us how far n is from the next multiple of k.

Case 1: If n is divisible by k (n % k == 0)
- Then n itself is a multiple of k, but we need something greater than n
- The next multiple would be (n ÷ k + 1) × k = (quotient + 1) × k

Case 2: If n is not divisible by k (n % k > 0)
- The next multiple of k is (n ÷ k + 1) × k = (quotient + 1) × k

Actually, we can simplify this:
- The smallest multiple of k that is greater than n is: `((n // k) + 1) * k`

Wait, let me verify with examples:

- (1 + 1) * 3 = 6 ✓

- 25 // 13 = 1
- (1 + 1) * 13 = 26 ✓

Example 3: n=26, k=13
- 26 // 13 = 2
- (2 + 1) * 13 = 39 ✓

Perfect! This formula works for all cases.

## Algorithm

1. Read n and k
2. Calculate quotient = n // k
3. Calculate result = (quotient + 1) * k
4. Output the result

## Edge Cases

- When n is exactly divisible by k: n % k == 0, we still get the correct result
- When n is negative: but constraints say n ≥ 1
- When k = 1: the answer is n + 1

All cases handled correctly by the formula.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the smallest integer x that is:
1. Greater than n (x > n)
2. Divisible by k (x % k == 0)

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me think about this mathematically:

If we have a number n and want to find the smallest number greater than n that is divisible by k, we can approach this in different ways:

**Method 1: Find remainder and adjust**
- If n is divisible by k (n % k == 0), then the next multiple would be n + k
- If n is not divisible by k, then we need to find the next multiple after n

**Method 2: Using division**
- We can divide n by k to get the quotient
- The next multiple of k after n would be: k * (quotient + 1)

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1: n=5, k=3**
- 5 % 3 = 2 (not divisible)
- We need to find the next multiple of 3 after 5
- 3 * 2 = 6 (which is > 5)
- Answer: 6

**Example 2: n=25, k=13**
- 25 % 13 = 12 (not divisible)
- Next multiple of 13 after 25
- 13 * 2 = 26 (which is > 25)
- Answer: 26

**Example 3: n=26, k=13**
- 26 % 13 = 0 (divisible)
- Since 26 is divisible by 13, the next multiple is 26 + 13 = 39
- Answer: 39

## Algorithm

The general approach:
1. Calculate the quotient when n is divided by k: quotient = n // k
2. The next multiple after n is: k * (quotient + 1)
3. This will always be greater than n and divisible by k

## Verification

Let me verify with the examples:
- n=5, k=3: 5//3 = 1, so answer = 3*(1+1) = 6 ✓
- n=25, k=13: 25//13 = 1, so answer = 13*(1+1) = 26 ✓  
- n=26, k=13: 26//13 = 2, so answer = 13*(2+1) = 39 ✓

## Edge Cases

What if n is exactly divisible by k? Our formula still works:
- n = k*m, then n//k = m, so answer = k*(m+1) = n + k

What if n < k? 
- n//k = 0, so answer = k*(0+1) = k
- Since k > n and k is divisible by k, this is correct.

## Code Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate the next multiple of k that is greater than n
quotient = n // k
next_multiple = k * (quotient + 1)

print(next_multiple)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the smallest integer x that is:
1. Greater than n (x > n)
2. Divisible by k (x % k == 0)

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me think about this mathematically:

1. We want the smallest multiple of k that is greater than n.
2. The multiples of k are: k, 2k, 3k, 4k, ...
3. We need to find the smallest multiple of k that is > n.

## Approach

The key insight is:
- If n is divisible by k (n % k == 0), then the next multiple is (n // k + 1) * k
- If n is not divisible by k (n % k != 0), then the next multiple is (n // k + 1) * k

Actually, let me simplify this:
- We can find how many times k fits into n: n // k
- The next multiple after n would be (n // k + 1) * k

But we need to be careful about edge cases.

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** n=5, k=3
- 5 // 3 = 1
- (1 + 1) * 3 = 6 ✓

**Example 2:** n=25, k=13  
- 25 // 13 = 1
- (1 + 1) * 13 = 26 ✓

**Example 3:** n=26, k=13
- 26 // 13 = 2
- (2 + 1) * 13 = 39 ✓

Perfect! This works for all cases.

## Algorithm

1. Read n and k
2. Calculate quotient = n // k
3. Calculate result = (quotient + 1) * k
4. Return result

## Edge Cases

- When n is exactly divisible by k: n = m * k, then next multiple is (m + 1) * k
- When n is not divisible by k: we still use the same formula

The formula works because:
- n // k gives us the number of complete multiples of k that fit into n
- Adding 1 and multiplying by k gives us the next multiple of k

## Code Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())
result = ((n // k) + 1) * k
print(result)
```

The solution is elegant and handles all cases correctly. The time complexity is O(1) and space complexity is O(1).

## Problem Understanding

We need to find the smallest integer x that is:
1. Greater than n (x > n)
2. Divisible by k (x % k == 0)

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let's think about what this means:

If we have a number n and want the next multiple of k that's greater than n:

1. First, let's find what multiple of k we're currently at or before n.
2. If n is divisible by k, then the next multiple would be n + k.
3. If n is not divisible by k, then we need to find the next multiple of k.

## Mathematical Approach

The key insight is:
- If n is divisible by k (n % k == 0), then the answer is n + k
- If n is not divisible by k (n % k != 0), then we need to find the next multiple of k

We can calculate this as:
- The next multiple of k after n is: (n // k + 1) * k

But wait, let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=5, k=3
- 5 % 3 = 2 ≠ 0, so not divisible
- (5 // 3 + 1) * 3 = (1 + 1) * 3 = 6 ✓

- (25 // 13 + 1) * 13 = (1 + 1) * 13 = 26 ✓

Example 3: n=26, k=13
- 26 % 13 = 0, so divisible
- (26 // 13 + 1) * 13 = (2 + 1) * 13 = 39 ✓

Perfect! The formula works for all cases.

## Algorithm

2. Calculate the next multiple of k that is greater than n using: `(n // k + 1) * k`
3. Output the result

## Edge Cases

The formula works even when n is exactly divisible by k:
- If n = 26, k = 13: (26 // 13 + 1) * 13 = (2 + 1) * 13 = 39

This matches the requirement of being strictly greater than n.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Your task in this problem is to create a program that finds the shortest path between two given locations on a given street map, which is represented as a collection of line segments on a plane.

<image>

Figure 4 is an example of a street map, where some line segments represent streets and the others are signs indicating the directions in which cars cannot move. More concretely, AE, AM, MQ, EQ, CP and HJ represent the streets and the others are signs in this map. In general, an end point of a sign touches one and only one line segment representing a street and the other end point is open. Each end point of every street touches one or more streets, but no signs.

The sign BF, for instance, indicates that at B cars may move left to right but may not in the reverse direction. In general, cars may not move from the obtuse angle side to the acute angle side at a point where a sign touches a street (note that the angle CBF is obtuse and the angle ABF is acute). Cars may directly move neither from P to M nor from M to P since cars moving left to right may not go through N and those moving right to left may not go through O. In a special case where the angle between a sign and a street is rectangular, cars may not move in either directions at the point. For instance, cars may directly move neither from H to J nor from J to H.

You should write a program that finds the shortest path obeying these traffic rules. The length of a line segment between (x1, y1) and (x2, y2) is √{(x2 − x1)2 + (y2 − y1 )2} .



Input

The input consists of multiple datasets, each in the following format.

xg yg
x11 y11 x21 y21

x1k y1k x2k y2k
.

.
x1n y1n x2n y2n


n, representing the number of line segments, is a positive integer less than or equal to 200.

(xs, ys) and (xg, yg) are the start and goal points, respectively. You can assume that (xs, ys) ≠ (xg, yg) and that each of them is located on an end point of some line segment representing a street. You can also assume that the shortest path from (xs, ys) to (xg, yg) is unique.

(x1k, y1k) and (x2k, y2k ) are the two end points of the kth line segment. You can assume that (x1k, y1k) ≠(x2k, y2k ). Two line segments never cross nor overlap. That is, if they share a point, it is always one of their end points.

All the coordinates are non-negative integers less than or equal to 1000. The end of the input is indicated by a line containing a single zero.

Output

For each input dataset, print every street intersection point on the shortest path from the start point to the goal point, one in an output line in this order, and a zero in a line following those points. Note that a street intersection point is a point where at least two line segments representing streets meet. An output line for a street intersection point should contain its x- and y-coordinates separated by a space.

Print -1 if there are no paths from the start point to the goal point.
